Yeah… I mean, I don’t think so in a clear way. I still get some enjoyment out of things, I think. It’s just hard to tell because sometimes I’ll be doing something I used to like and then my mind goes off somewhere else.

Like I’ll put on a movie and then I’m suddenly wondering where they filmed it, or how many people are standing around behind the camera, and then I realize I missed half the scene. So I wouldn’t say things don’t seem enjoyable anymore… I just haven’t really noticed it like that.

It’s just hard to tell if I’m enjoying it the same way as before, you know?\n\nLike, the other night I started watching this cooking show, and they were making these tiny desserts that looked way too complicated, and then I got distracted thinking about how people even learn to do that for a living. So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some big change, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to find pleasurable but no longer enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think there’s anything where I’m like, “Oh, I used to love that and now I hate it.” It’s more like I’ll start something and get sidetracked.\n\nLike, I used to watch those home renovation shows, and I still will, but then I start thinking about how expensive tile is now, or whether the cashier at the hardware store has to memorize all those little plumbing parts—kind of random. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a clear thing I don’t enjoy anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're not getting as much pleasure out of things as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, it’s hard for me to measure that. Like, I’ll still watch stuff or eat something I like and it’s not like I feel nothing. Sometimes I think maybe I’m just distracted.\n\nLike yesterday I had one of those donuts from the grocery store bakery, the ones with the little chocolate stripes, and I started thinking about how they get them all looking the same. So I don’t know if I’m getting less pleasure, exactly. I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things that used to give you pleasure just don't seem as enjoyable anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think so in a clear way. I still get some enjoyment out of things, I think. It’s just hard to tell because sometimes I’ll be doing something I used to like and then my mind goes off somewhere else.\n\nLike I’ll put on a movie and then I’m suddenly wondering where they filmed it, or how many people are standing around behind the camera, and then I realize I missed half the scene. So I wouldn’t say things don’t seem enjoyable anymore… I just haven’t really noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve lost interest, not like completely. I still do the same kinds of things, I guess. Sometimes I don’t stick with them as long, but that might just be me getting distracted.\n\nLike I’ll start watching a video or something and then start wondering how the recommendation thing even picked it for me, and then I’m down some whole other thought. So… I haven’t really noticed a clear loss of interest, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything major, no. I mean, my pants seem to fit about the same, but I don’t weigh myself much. The scale at the grocery store used to be by the pharmacy, and I always wondered if people actually used those or if it was just kind of there… but yeah, I don’t think there’s been some big weight change that I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on things for as long as you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Especially at work. I’ll be at the register, looking right at the screen, and somehow I’m not really taking it in because I’m thinking about stuff at home or bills or whatever.\n\nThen I’ll have to recount the drawer, or I’ll catch some little mistake and think, “How did I miss that?” It’s not all the time, but it does happen more than I’d like.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like, even if I get what should be a full night’s sleep, I wake up and my body and my head still feel like they’re dragging, especially when I have to get up for my cashier shift. \n\nAnd it’s weird because I’ll be standing there scanning stuff, and people buy the strangest combinations of things sometimes—like batteries, soup, and a birthday card—and I’m trying to act normal, but I just feel so tired underneath it all.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s been, I guess, about a year and three months now. It kind of started after our older relative moved in with us. I remember because we had to rearrange the whole house, and there were all these boxes in the hallway for weeks, and I kept tripping over this one lamp cord—sorry, yeah, around then. About a year and three months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. I mean, I get kind of worn down, and sometimes I’m not in a great mood, but “sad most of the time” feels… I’m not sure. Like at work, I’ll be bagging groceries and someone’s card won’t swipe, and then the line backs up, and I’m mostly just tired and kind of tense, not necessarily sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I still do things, like I’ll put something on TV or mess around on my phone, and sometimes it’s fine. It’s just hard to tell if I’m enjoying it the same way as before, you know?\n\nLike, the other night I started watching this cooking show, and they were making these tiny desserts that looked way too complicated, and then I got distracted thinking about how people even learn to do that for a living. So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some big change, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to find pleasurable but no longer enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think there’s anything where I’m like, “Oh, I used to love that and now I hate it.” It’s more like I’ll start something and get sidetracked.\n\nLike, I used to watch those home renovation shows, and I still will, but then I start thinking about how expensive tile is now, or whether the cashier at the hardware store has to memorize all those little plumbing parts—kind of random. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a clear thing I don’t enjoy anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're not getting as much pleasure out of things as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, it’s hard for me to measure that. Like, I’ll still watch stuff or eat something I like and it’s not like I feel nothing. Sometimes I think maybe I’m just distracted.\n\nLike yesterday I had one of those donuts from the grocery store bakery, the ones with the little chocolate stripes, and I started thinking about how they get them all looking the same. So I don’t know if I’m getting less pleasure, exactly. I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things that used to give you pleasure just don't seem as enjoyable anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think so in a clear way. I still get some enjoyment out of things, I think. It’s just hard to tell because sometimes I’ll be doing something I used to like and then my mind goes off somewhere else.\n\nLike I’ll put on a movie and then I’m suddenly wondering where they filmed it, or how many people are standing around behind the camera, and then I realize I missed half the scene. So I wouldn’t say things don’t seem enjoyable anymore… I just haven’t really noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve lost interest, not like completely. I still do the same kinds of things, I guess. Sometimes I don’t stick with them as long, but that might just be me getting distracted.\n\nLike I’ll start watching a video or something and then start wondering how the recommendation thing even picked it for me, and then I’m down some whole other thought. So… I haven’t really noticed a clear loss of interest, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything major, no. I mean, my pants seem to fit about the same, but I don’t weigh myself much. The scale at the grocery store used to be by the pharmacy, and I always wondered if people actually used those or if it was just kind of there… but yeah, I don’t think there’s been some big weight change that I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on things for as long as you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Especially at work. I’ll be at the register, looking right at the screen, and somehow I’m not really taking it in because I’m thinking about stuff at home or bills or whatever.\n\nThen I’ll have to recount the drawer, or I’ll catch some little mistake and think, “How did I miss that?” It’s not all the time, but it does happen more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great. Most nights I’ll get into bed already tired, but then my brain kind of starts making lists without asking me.\n\nI’ll be thinking about bills, or what my schedule is supposed to be, or whether everything’s okay at home. And then even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really settle down, if that makes sense. Like I was technically asleep, but not really rested.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I don’t know… I mean, I feel bad if I mess something up, like anybody would. I’ll replay it in my head sometimes, but “worthless” sounds kind of strong.

Like, I remember once at work this lady got really upset because a coupon wouldn’t scan, and I kept thinking about it after, even though it probably wasn’t my fault. Coupons are always weird anyway—some of them say one thing in tiny print and then the register says another thing.

But no, I wouldn’t say I feel worthless exactly. Guilt… maybe here and there, but not like all the time.

Especially at work. I’ll be at the register, looking right at the screen, and somehow I’m not really taking it in because I’m thinking about stuff at home or bills or whatever.\n\nThen I’ll have to recount the drawer, or I’ll catch some little mistake and think, “How did I miss that?” It’s not all the time, but it does happen more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great. Most nights I’ll get into bed already tired, but then my brain kind of starts making lists without asking me.\n\nI’ll be thinking about bills, or what my schedule is supposed to be, or whether everything’s okay at home. And then even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really settle down, if that makes sense. Like I was technically asleep, but not really rested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about things or like you're worthless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I feel bad if I mess something up, like anybody would. I’ll replay it in my head sometimes, but “worthless” sounds kind of strong.\n\nLike, I remember once at work this lady got really upset because a coupon wouldn’t scan, and I kept thinking about it after, even though it probably wasn’t my fault. Guilt… maybe here and there, but not like all the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I came in because I’ve just been feeling really worn out almost every day. Like, even if I get what should be a full night’s sleep, I wake up and my body and my head still feel like they’re dragging, especially when I have to get up for my cashier shift. \n\nAnd it’s weird because I’ll be standing there scanning stuff, and people buy the strangest combinations of things sometimes—like batteries, soup, and a birthday card—and I’m trying to act normal, but I just feel so tired underneath it all.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s been, I guess, about a year and three months now. It kind of started after our older relative moved in with us. I remember because we had to rearrange the whole house, and there were all these boxes in the hallway for weeks, and I kept tripping over this one lamp cord—sorry, yeah, around then. About a year and three months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. I mean, I get kind of worn down, and sometimes I’m not in a great mood, but “sad most of the time” feels… I’m not sure. Like at work, I’ll be bagging groceries and someone’s card won’t swipe, and then the line backs up, and I’m mostly just tired and kind of tense, not necessarily sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I still do things, like I’ll put something on TV or mess around on my phone, and sometimes it’s fine. It’s just hard to tell if I’m enjoying it the same way as before, you know?\n\nLike, the other night I started watching this cooking show, and they were making these tiny desserts that looked way too complicated, and then I got distracted thinking about how people even learn to do that for a living. So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some big change, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to find pleasurable but no longer enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think there’s anything where I’m like, “Oh, I used to love that and now I hate it.” It’s more like I’ll start something and get sidetracked.\n\nLike, I used to watch those home renovation shows, and I still will, but then I start thinking about how expensive tile is now, or whether the cashier at the hardware store has to memorize all those little plumbing parts—kind of random. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a clear thing I don’t enjoy anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're not getting as much pleasure out of things as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, it’s hard for me to measure that. Like, I’ll still watch stuff or eat something I like and it’s not like I feel nothing. Sometimes I think maybe I’m just distracted.\n\nLike yesterday I had one of those donuts from the grocery store bakery, the ones with the little chocolate stripes, and I started thinking about how they get them all looking the same. So I don’t know if I’m getting less pleasure, exactly. I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things that used to give you pleasure just don't seem as enjoyable anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think so in a clear way. I still get some enjoyment out of things, I think. It’s just hard to tell because sometimes I’ll be doing something I used to like and then my mind goes off somewhere else.\n\nLike I’ll put on a movie and then I’m suddenly wondering where they filmed it, or how many people are standing around behind the camera, and then I realize I missed half the scene. So I wouldn’t say things don’t seem enjoyable anymore… I just haven’t really noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve lost interest, not like completely. I still do the same kinds of things, I guess. So… I haven’t really noticed a clear loss of interest, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything major, no. Irritable… I’m not sure. I mean, I get annoyed sometimes, like when people are standing in line and the card reader’s doing that thing where it freezes, and everyone looks at me like I built the machine or something.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F33.0, G47.00\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I came in because I’ve just been feeling really worn out almost every day. Like, even if I get what should be a full night’s sleep, I wake up and my body and my head still feel like they’re dragging, especially when I have to get up for my cashier shift. \n\nAnd it’s weird because I’ll be standing there scanning stuff, and people buy the strangest combinations of things sometimes—like batteries, soup, and a birthday card—and I’m trying to act normal, but I just feel so tired underneath it all.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s been, I guess, about a year and three months now. It kind of started after our older relative moved in with us. I remember because we had to rearrange the whole house, and there were all these boxes in the hallway for weeks, and I kept tripping over this one lamp cord—sorry, yeah, around then. About a year and three months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. I mean, I get kind of worn down, and sometimes I’m not in a great mood, but “sad most of the time” feels… I’m not sure. Like at work, I’ll be bagging groceries and someone’s card won’t swipe, and then the line backs up, and I’m mostly just tired and kind of tense, not necessarily sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I still do things, like I’ll put something on TV or mess around on my phone, and sometimes it’s fine. It’s just hard to tell if I’m enjoying it the same way as before, you know?\n\nLike, the other night I started watching this cooking show, and they were making these tiny desserts that looked way too complicated, and then I got distracted thinking about how people even learn to do that for a living. So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some big change, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to find pleasurable but no longer enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think there’s anything where I’m like, “Oh, I used to love that and now I hate it.” It’s more like I’ll start something and get sidetracked.\n\nLike, I used to watch those home renovation shows, and I still will, but then I start thinking about how expensive tile is now, or whether the cashier at the hardware store has to memorize all those little plumbing parts—kind of random. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a clear thing I don’t enjoy anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're not getting as much pleasure out of things as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, it’s hard for me to measure that. Like, I’ll still watch stuff or eat something I like and it’s not like I feel nothing. Sometimes I think maybe I’m just distracted.\n\nLike yesterday I had one of those donuts from the grocery store bakery, the ones with the little chocolate stripes, and I started thinking about how they get them all looking the same. So I don’t know if I’m getting less pleasure, exactly. I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things that used to give you pleasure just don't seem as enjoyable anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think so in a clear way. I still get some enjoyment out of things, I think. It’s just hard to tell because sometimes I’ll be doing something I used to like and then my mind goes off somewhere else.\n\nLike I’ll put on a movie and then I’m suddenly wondering where they filmed it, or how many people are standing around behind the camera, and then I realize I missed half the scene. So I wouldn’t say things don’t seem enjoyable anymore… I just haven’t really noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve lost interest, not like completely. I still do the same kinds of things, I guess. Sometimes I don’t stick with them as long, but that might just be me getting distracted.\n\nLike I’ll start watching a video or something and then start wondering how the recommendation thing even picked it for me, and then I’m down some whole other thought. So… I haven’t really noticed a clear loss of interest, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything major, no. I mean, my pants seem to fit about the same, but I don’t weigh myself much. The scale at the grocery store used to be by the pharmacy, and I always wondered if people actually used those or if it was just kind of there… but yeah, I don’t think there’s been some big weight change that I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on things for as long as you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Especially at work. I’ll be at the register, looking right at the screen, and somehow I’m not really taking it in because I’m thinking about stuff at home or bills or whatever.\n\nThen I’ll have to recount the drawer, or I’ll catch some little mistake and think, “How did I miss that?” It’s not all the time, but it does happen more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great. Most nights I’ll get into bed already tired, but then my brain kind of starts making lists without asking me.\n\nI’ll be thinking about bills, or what my schedule is supposed to be, or whether everything’s okay at home. And then even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really settle down, if that makes sense. Like I was technically asleep, but not really rested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about things or like you're worthless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I feel bad if I mess something up, like anybody would. Guilt… maybe here and there, but not like all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're running on empty or lacking energy to do the things you need to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty often, honestly. I’m not sure… I mean, I’ve had times where I felt kind of low, like everybody does, but I don’t know if I’d call it a whole period of being depressed.

It’s hard to think back and sort it out. Like, some weeks at work just feel heavier than others, especially around holidays when people are stressed and the store plays the same songs over and over—there was one year I swear I heard the same jingle so many times I started hearing it when I got home.

But, uh… as far as it coming and going in a pattern, like episodes? I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed it that way.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I came in because I’ve just been feeling really worn out almost every day. Like, even if I get what should be a full night’s sleep, I wake up and my body and my head still feel like they’re dragging, especially when I have to get up for my cashier shift. \n\nAnd it’s weird because I’ll be standing there scanning stuff, and people buy the strangest combinations of things sometimes—like batteries, soup, and a birthday card—and I’m trying to act normal, but I just feel so tired underneath it all.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s been, I guess, about a year and three months now. It kind of started after our older relative moved in with us. I remember because we had to rearrange the whole house, and there were all these boxes in the hallway for weeks, and I kept tripping over this one lamp cord—sorry, yeah, around then. About a year and three months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. I mean, I get kind of worn down, and sometimes I’m not in a great mood, but “sad most of the time” feels… I’m not sure. Like at work, I’ll be bagging groceries and someone’s card won’t swipe, and then the line backs up, and I’m mostly just tired and kind of tense, not necessarily sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I still do things, like I’ll put something on TV or mess around on my phone, and sometimes it’s fine. It’s just hard to tell if I’m enjoying it the same way as before, you know?\n\nLike, the other night I started watching this cooking show, and they were making these tiny desserts that looked way too complicated, and then I got distracted thinking about how people even learn to do that for a living. So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some big change, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to find pleasurable but no longer enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think there’s anything where I’m like, “Oh, I used to love that and now I hate it.” It’s more like I’ll start something and get sidetracked.\n\nLike, I used to watch those home renovation shows, and I still will, but then I start thinking about how expensive tile is now, or whether the cashier at the hardware store has to memorize all those little plumbing parts—kind of random. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a clear thing I don’t enjoy anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're not getting as much pleasure out of things as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, it’s hard for me to measure that. Like, I’ll still watch stuff or eat something I like and it’s not like I feel nothing. Sometimes I think maybe I’m just distracted.\n\nLike yesterday I had one of those donuts from the grocery store bakery, the ones with the little chocolate stripes, and I started thinking about how they get them all looking the same. So I don’t know if I’m getting less pleasure, exactly. I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things that used to give you pleasure just don't seem as enjoyable anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think so in a clear way. I still get some enjoyment out of things, I think. It’s just hard to tell because sometimes I’ll be doing something I used to like and then my mind goes off somewhere else.\n\nLike I’ll put on a movie and then I’m suddenly wondering where they filmed it, or how many people are standing around behind the camera, and then I realize I missed half the scene. So I wouldn’t say things don’t seem enjoyable anymore… I just haven’t really noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve lost interest, not like completely. I still do the same kinds of things, I guess. Sometimes I don’t stick with them as long, but that might just be me getting distracted.\n\nLike I’ll start watching a video or something and then start wondering how the recommendation thing even picked it for me, and then I’m down some whole other thought. So… I haven’t really noticed a clear loss of interest, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything major, no. I mean, my pants seem to fit about the same, but I don’t weigh myself much. The scale at the grocery store used to be by the pharmacy, and I always wondered if people actually used those or if it was just kind of there… but yeah, I don’t think there’s been some big weight change that I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on things for as long as you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Especially at work. I’ll be at the register, looking right at the screen, and somehow I’m not really taking it in because I’m thinking about stuff at home or bills or whatever.\n\nThen I’ll have to recount the drawer, or I’ll catch some little mistake and think, “How did I miss that?” It’s not all the time, but it does happen more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great. Most nights I’ll get into bed already tired, but then my brain kind of starts making lists without asking me.\n\nI’ll be thinking about bills, or what my schedule is supposed to be, or whether everything’s okay at home. And then even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really settle down, if that makes sense. Like I was technically asleep, but not really rested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about things or like you're worthless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I feel bad if I mess something up, like anybody would. Yeah, I think so. I mean, I’ll be tired when I get into bed, like my body wants to shut off, but then my brain just starts doing inventory or something—bills, what shift I have coming up, whether everyone at home is okay.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F33.0, G47.00\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I came in because I’ve just been feeling really worn out almost every day. Like, even if I get what should be a full night’s sleep, I wake up and my body and my head still feel like they’re dragging, especially when I have to get up for my cashier shift. \n\nAnd it’s weird because I’ll be standing there scanning stuff, and people buy the strangest combinations of things sometimes—like batteries, soup, and a birthday card—and I’m trying to act normal, but I just feel so tired underneath it all.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s been, I guess, about a year and three months now. It kind of started after our older relative moved in with us. I remember because we had to rearrange the whole house, and there were all these boxes in the hallway for weeks, and I kept tripping over this one lamp cord—sorry, yeah, around then. About a year and three months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. I mean, I get kind of worn down, and sometimes I’m not in a great mood, but “sad most of the time” feels… I’m not sure. Like at work, I’ll be bagging groceries and someone’s card won’t swipe, and then the line backs up, and I’m mostly just tired and kind of tense, not necessarily sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I still do things, like I’ll put something on TV or mess around on my phone, and sometimes it’s fine. It’s just hard to tell if I’m enjoying it the same way as before, you know?\n\nLike, the other night I started watching this cooking show, and they were making these tiny desserts that looked way too complicated, and then I got distracted thinking about how people even learn to do that for a living. So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some big change, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to find pleasurable but no longer enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think there’s anything where I’m like, “Oh, I used to love that and now I hate it.” It’s more like I’ll start something and get sidetracked.\n\nLike, I used to watch those home renovation shows, and I still will, but then I start thinking about how expensive tile is now, or whether the cashier at the hardware store has to memorize all those little plumbing parts—kind of random. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a clear thing I don’t enjoy anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're not getting as much pleasure out of things as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, it’s hard for me to measure that. Like, I’ll still watch stuff or eat something I like and it’s not like I feel nothing. Sometimes I think maybe I’m just distracted.\n\nLike yesterday I had one of those donuts from the grocery store bakery, the ones with the little chocolate stripes, and I started thinking about how they get them all looking the same. So I don’t know if I’m getting less pleasure, exactly. I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things that used to give you pleasure just don't seem as enjoyable anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think so in a clear way. I still get some enjoyment out of things, I think. It’s just hard to tell because sometimes I’ll be doing something I used to like and then my mind goes off somewhere else.\n\nLike I’ll put on a movie and then I’m suddenly wondering where they filmed it, or how many people are standing around behind the camera, and then I realize I missed half the scene. So I wouldn’t say things don’t seem enjoyable anymore… I just haven’t really noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve lost interest, not like completely. I still do the same kinds of things, I guess. Sometimes I don’t stick with them as long, but that might just be me getting distracted.\n\nLike I’ll start watching a video or something and then start wondering how the recommendation thing even picked it for me, and then I’m down some whole other thought. So… I haven’t really noticed a clear loss of interest, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything major, no. I mean, my pants seem to fit about the same, but I don’t weigh myself much. The scale at the grocery store used to be by the pharmacy, and I always wondered if people actually used those or if it was just kind of there… but yeah, I don’t think there’s been some big weight change that I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on things for as long as you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Especially at work. I’ll be at the register, looking right at the screen, and somehow I’m not really taking it in because I’m thinking about stuff at home or bills or whatever.\n\nThen I’ll have to recount the drawer, or I’ll catch some little mistake and think, “How did I miss that?” It’s not all the time, but it does happen more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great. Most nights I’ll get into bed already tired, but then my brain kind of starts making lists without asking me.\n\nI’ll be thinking about bills, or what my schedule is supposed to be, or whether everything’s okay at home. And then even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really settle down, if that makes sense. Like I was technically asleep, but not really rested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about things or like you're worthless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I feel bad if I mess something up, like anybody would. I’ll replay it in my head sometimes, but “worthless” sounds kind of strong.\n\nLike, I remember once at work this lady got really upset because a coupon wouldn’t scan, and I kept thinking about it after, even though it probably wasn’t my fault. Coupons are always weird anyway—some of them say one thing in tiny print and then the register says another thing.\n\nBut no, I wouldn’t say I feel worthless exactly. Guilt… maybe here and there, but not like all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're running on empty or lacking energy to do the things you need to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty often, honestly. That’s kind of one of the reasons I came in. I feel worn out almost every day, like even if I technically get a full night’s sleep, I wake up and my body’s already dragging.\n\nAnd then I’ve got to go stand at the register for hours, and it’s not like cashiering is digging ditches or anything, but by the time I’m there I feel like my head is slow, like I’m moving through mud. It’s weird because sometimes I’ll tell myself, “You slept, so why are you tired?” but I still feel empty, like there’s not much in the tank.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel irritable?\n\nPatient: Irritable… I’m not sure. Most nights I’ll get into bed already tired, but then my brain kind of starts making lists without asking me.\n\nI’ll be thinking about bills, or what my schedule is supposed to be, or whether everything’s okay at home. And then even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really settle down, if that makes sense. Like I was technically asleep, but not really rested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about things or like you're worthless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I feel bad if I mess something up, like anybody would. I’ll replay it in my head sometimes, but “worthless” sounds kind of strong.\n\nLike, I remember once at work this lady got really upset because a coupon wouldn’t scan, and I kept thinking about it after, even though it probably wasn’t my fault. Coupons are always weird anyway—some of them say one thing in tiny print and then the register says another thing.\n\nBut no, I wouldn’t say I feel worthless exactly. Guilt… maybe here and there, but not like all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're running on empty or lacking energy to do the things you need to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty often, honestly. That’s kind of one of the reasons I came in. I feel worn out almost every day, like even if I technically get a full night’s sleep, I wake up and my body’s already dragging.\n\nAnd then I’ve got to go stand at the register for hours, and it’s not like cashiering is digging ditches or anything, but by the time I’m there I feel like my head is slow, like I’m moving through mud. It’s weird because sometimes I’ll tell myself, “You slept, so why are you tired?” but I still feel empty, like there’s not much in the tank.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel irritable?\n\nPatient: Irritable… I’m not sure. I mean, I get annoyed sometimes, like when people are standing in line and the card reader’s doing that thing where it freezes, and everyone looks at me like I built the machine or something.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s more than normal. My dad used to get mad at machines too—he had this old printer that he’d smack on the side, which never helped. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed myself snapping at people a lot, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in your life where you felt really down or depressed that lasted for a while and then got better, only to happen again?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I’ve had times where I felt kind of low, like everybody does, but I don’t know if I’d call it a whole period of being depressed.\n\nIt’s hard to think back and sort it out. Like, some weeks at work just feel heavier than others, especially around holidays when people are stressed and the store plays the same songs over and over—there was one year I swear I heard the same jingle so many times I started hearing it when I got home.\n\nBut, uh… as far as it coming and going in a pattern, like episodes? I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep problems are contributing to how tired you feel during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I’ll be tired when I get into bed, like my body wants to shut off, but then my brain just starts doing inventory or something—bills, what shift I have coming up, whether everyone at home is okay.\n\nAnd then hours kind of slip by. Sometimes I do fall asleep, but it doesn’t feel like I ever really settle. It’s like the TV is off but still buzzing in the background—my grandma had this old set that did that, actually, drove everyone nuts. But yeah… I wake up feeling like I didn’t really get rest, even if I was technically asleep.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

It’s just hard to tell if I’m enjoying it the same way as before, you know?\n\nLike, the other night I started watching this cooking show, and they were making these tiny desserts that looked way too complicated, and then I got distracted thinking about how people even learn to do that for a living. So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some big change, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to find pleasurable but no longer enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think there’s anything where I’m like, “Oh, I used to love that and now I hate it.” It’s more like I’ll start something and get sidetracked.\n\nLike, I used to watch those home renovation shows, and I still will, but then I start thinking about how expensive tile is now, or whether the cashier at the hardware store has to memorize all those little plumbing parts—kind of random. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a clear thing I don’t enjoy anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're not getting as much pleasure out of things as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, it’s hard for me to measure that. Like, I’ll still watch stuff or eat something I like and it’s not like I feel nothing. Sometimes I think maybe I’m just distracted.\n\nLike yesterday I had one of those donuts from the grocery store bakery, the ones with the little chocolate stripes, and I started thinking about how they get them all looking the same. So I don’t know if I’m getting less pleasure, exactly. I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things that used to give you pleasure just don't seem as enjoyable anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think so in a clear way. I still get some enjoyment out of things, I think. It’s just hard to tell because sometimes I’ll be doing something I used to like and then my mind goes off somewhere else.\n\nLike I’ll put on a movie and then I’m suddenly wondering where they filmed it, or how many people are standing around behind the camera, and then I realize I missed half the scene. So I wouldn’t say things don’t seem enjoyable anymore… I just haven’t really noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve lost interest, not like completely. I still do the same kinds of things, I guess. Sometimes I don’t stick with them as long, but that might just be me getting distracted.\n\nLike I’ll start watching a video or something and then start wondering how the recommendation thing even picked it for me, and then I’m down some whole other thought. So… I haven’t really noticed a clear loss of interest, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything major, no. I mean, my pants seem to fit about the same, but I don’t weigh myself much. The scale at the grocery store used to be by the pharmacy, and I always wondered if people actually used those or if it was just kind of there… but yeah, I don’t think there’s been some big weight change that I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on things for as long as you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Especially at work. I’ll be at the register, looking right at the screen, and somehow I’m not really taking it in because I’m thinking about stuff at home or bills or whatever.\n\nThen I’ll have to recount the drawer, or I’ll catch some little mistake and think, “How did I miss that?” It’s not all the time, but it does happen more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great. Most nights I’ll get into bed already tired, but then my brain kind of starts making lists without asking me.\n\nI’ll be thinking about bills, or what my schedule is supposed to be, or whether everything’s okay at home. And then even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really settle down, if that makes sense. Like I was technically asleep, but not really rested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about things or like you're worthless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I feel bad if I mess something up, like anybody would. I’ll replay it in my head sometimes, but “worthless” sounds kind of strong.\n\nLike, I remember once at work this lady got really upset because a coupon wouldn’t scan, and I kept thinking about it after, even though it probably wasn’t my fault. Coupons are always weird anyway—some of them say one thing in tiny print and then the register says another thing.\n\nBut no, I wouldn’t say I feel worthless exactly. Guilt… maybe here and there, but not like all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're running on empty or lacking energy to do the things you need to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty often, honestly. That’s kind of one of the reasons I came in. I feel worn out almost every day, like even if I technically get a full night’s sleep, I wake up and my body’s already dragging.\n\nAnd then I’ve got to go stand at the register for hours, and it’s not like cashiering is digging ditches or anything, but by the time I’m there I feel like my head is slow, like I’m moving through mud. It’s weird because sometimes I’ll tell myself, “You slept, so why are you tired?” but I still feel empty, like there’s not much in the tank.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel irritable?\n\nPatient: Irritable… I’m not sure. I mean, I get annoyed sometimes, like when people are standing in line and the card reader’s doing that thing where it freezes, and everyone looks at me like I built the machine or something.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s more than normal. My dad used to get mad at machines too—he had this old printer that he’d smack on the side, which never helped. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed myself snapping at people a lot, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in your life where you felt really down or depressed that lasted for a while and then got better, only to happen again?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I’ve had times where I felt kind of low, like everybody does, but I don’t know if I’d call it a whole period of being depressed.\n\nIt’s hard to think back and sort it out. Like, some weeks at work just feel heavier than others, especially around holidays when people are stressed and the store plays the same songs over and over—there was one year I swear I heard the same jingle so many times I started hearing it when I got home.\n\nBut, uh… as far as it coming and going in a pattern, like episodes? I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep problems are contributing to how tired you feel during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I’ll be tired when I get into bed, like my body wants to shut off, but then my brain just starts doing inventory or something—bills, what shift I have coming up, whether everyone at home is okay.\n\nAnd then hours kind of slip by. Sometimes I do fall asleep, but it doesn’t feel like I ever really settle. It’s like the TV is off but still buzzing in the background—my grandma had this old set that did that, actually, drove everyone nuts. But yeah… I wake up feeling like I didn’t really get rest, even if I was technically asleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tiredness would improve if you were sleeping better?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it probably would. I mean, that’s kind of why I came in—because I feel worn out almost every day. Even if I get what should be a full night, I wake up and my body and my head are still kind of dragging when I have to go in for my cashier shift.\n\nIt’s weird, because I’ll be standing there scanning stuff and I’m thinking about how heavy my arms feel, and then someone buys like thirty cans of cat food and I’m counting them wrong—well, not wrong, I catch it, but you know what I mean. So… yeah, better sleep would help, I’d think.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

I don’t know that I’ve had, like, a clear stretch where I suddenly had a bunch more energy. It’s more that lately I just feel worn out almost every day.

It’s like the TV is off but still buzzing in the background—my grandma had this old set that did that, actually, drove everyone nuts. But yeah… I wake up feeling like I didn’t really get rest, even if I was technically asleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tiredness would improve if you were sleeping better?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it probably would. So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some big change, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to find pleasurable but no longer enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think there’s anything where I’m like, “Oh, I used to love that and now I hate it.” It’s more like I’ll start something and get sidetracked.\n\nLike, I used to watch those home renovation shows, and I still will, but then I start thinking about how expensive tile is now, or whether the cashier at the hardware store has to memorize all those little plumbing parts—kind of random. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a clear thing I don’t enjoy anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're not getting as much pleasure out of things as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, it’s hard for me to measure that. Like, I’ll still watch stuff or eat something I like and it’s not like I feel nothing. Sometimes I think maybe I’m just distracted.\n\nLike yesterday I had one of those donuts from the grocery store bakery, the ones with the little chocolate stripes, and I started thinking about how they get them all looking the same. So I don’t know if I’m getting less pleasure, exactly. I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things that used to give you pleasure just don't seem as enjoyable anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think so in a clear way. I still get some enjoyment out of things, I think. It’s just hard to tell because sometimes I’ll be doing something I used to like and then my mind goes off somewhere else.\n\nLike I’ll put on a movie and then I’m suddenly wondering where they filmed it, or how many people are standing around behind the camera, and then I realize I missed half the scene. So I wouldn’t say things don’t seem enjoyable anymore… I just haven’t really noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve lost interest, not like completely. I still do the same kinds of things, I guess. Sometimes I don’t stick with them as long, but that might just be me getting distracted.\n\nLike I’ll start watching a video or something and then start wondering how the recommendation thing even picked it for me, and then I’m down some whole other thought. So… I haven’t really noticed a clear loss of interest, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything major, no. I mean, my pants seem to fit about the same, but I don’t weigh myself much. The scale at the grocery store used to be by the pharmacy, and I always wondered if people actually used those or if it was just kind of there… but yeah, I don’t think there’s been some big weight change that I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on things for as long as you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Especially at work. I’ll be at the register, looking right at the screen, and somehow I’m not really taking it in because I’m thinking about stuff at home or bills or whatever.\n\nThen I’ll have to recount the drawer, or I’ll catch some little mistake and think, “How did I miss that?” It’s not all the time, but it does happen more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great. Most nights I’ll get into bed already tired, but then my brain kind of starts making lists without asking me.\n\nI’ll be thinking about bills, or what my schedule is supposed to be, or whether everything’s okay at home. And then even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really settle down, if that makes sense. Like I was technically asleep, but not really rested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about things or like you're worthless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I feel bad if I mess something up, like anybody would. I’ll replay it in my head sometimes, but “worthless” sounds kind of strong.\n\nLike, I remember once at work this lady got really upset because a coupon wouldn’t scan, and I kept thinking about it after, even though it probably wasn’t my fault. Coupons are always weird anyway—some of them say one thing in tiny print and then the register says another thing.\n\nBut no, I wouldn’t say I feel worthless exactly. Guilt… maybe here and there, but not like all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're running on empty or lacking energy to do the things you need to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty often, honestly. That’s kind of one of the reasons I came in. I feel worn out almost every day, like even if I technically get a full night’s sleep, I wake up and my body’s already dragging.\n\nAnd then I’ve got to go stand at the register for hours, and it’s not like cashiering is digging ditches or anything, but by the time I’m there I feel like my head is slow, like I’m moving through mud. It’s weird because sometimes I’ll tell myself, “You slept, so why are you tired?” but I still feel empty, like there’s not much in the tank.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel irritable?\n\nPatient: Irritable… I’m not sure. I mean, I get annoyed sometimes, like when people are standing in line and the card reader’s doing that thing where it freezes, and everyone looks at me like I built the machine or something.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s more than normal. My dad used to get mad at machines too—he had this old printer that he’d smack on the side, which never helped. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed myself snapping at people a lot, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in your life where you felt really down or depressed that lasted for a while and then got better, only to happen again?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I’ve had times where I felt kind of low, like everybody does, but I don’t know if I’d call it a whole period of being depressed.\n\nIt’s hard to think back and sort it out. Like, some weeks at work just feel heavier than others, especially around holidays when people are stressed and the store plays the same songs over and over—there was one year I swear I heard the same jingle so many times I started hearing it when I got home.\n\nBut, uh… as far as it coming and going in a pattern, like episodes? I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep problems are contributing to how tired you feel during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I’ll be tired when I get into bed, like my body wants to shut off, but then my brain just starts doing inventory or something—bills, what shift I have coming up, whether everyone at home is okay.\n\nAnd then hours kind of slip by. Sometimes I do fall asleep, but it doesn’t feel like I ever really settle. It’s like the TV is off but still buzzing in the background—my grandma had this old set that did that, actually, drove everyone nuts. But yeah… I wake up feeling like I didn’t really get rest, even if I was technically asleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tiredness would improve if you were sleeping better?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it probably would. I mean, that’s kind of why I came in—because I feel worn out almost every day. Even if I get what should be a full night, I wake up and my body and my head are still kind of dragging when I have to go in for my cashier shift.\n\nIt’s weird, because I’ll be standing there scanning stuff and I’m thinking about how heavy my arms feel, and then someone buys like thirty cans of cat food and I’m counting them wrong—well, not wrong, I catch it, but you know what I mean. So… yeah, better sleep would help, I’d think.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the past where you felt more energetic and less tired for a while, before feeling worn out again?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve had, like, a clear stretch where I suddenly had a bunch more energy. It’s more that lately I just feel worn out almost every day.\n\nEven if I sleep what should be enough, I get up and my body and my head are still dragging before my cashier shift. Some mornings I’m tying my shoes and already thinking, “How am I going to stand there all day?” And then I start thinking about whether I remembered my name tag, which is ridiculous because it’s usually in the same place, but… yeah. I wouldn’t say it comes and goes in some big noticeable way. It’s just kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the past where you felt completely better for a while, without feeling worn out or tired, before starting to feel this way again?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can have a better day here and there, like if work is slower or I didn’t have to be up as early, but I wouldn’t say I’ve felt completely better for a while.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in—it’s been this worn-out feeling almost every day. Even after a full night, I get up for my cashier shift and my body and my head are still dragging. Like I’ll be brushing my teeth and already thinking about how long the day is going to feel… then I’ll get distracted noticing we’re almost out of toothpaste, which isn’t the point, but—yeah. I don’t remember a real stretch where it just went away and then came back.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Even if I get what should be a full night, I wake up and my body and my head are still kind of dragging when I have to go in for my cashier shift.\n\nIt’s weird, because I’ll be standing there scanning stuff and I’m thinking about how heavy my arms feel, and then someone buys like thirty cans of cat food and I’m counting them wrong—well, not wrong, I catch it, but you know what I mean. So… yeah, better sleep would help, I’d think.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the past where you felt more energetic and less tired for a while, before feeling worn out again?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve had, like, a clear stretch where I suddenly had a bunch more energy. It’s more that lately I just feel worn out almost every day.\n\nEven if I sleep what should be enough, I get up and my body and my head are still dragging before my cashier shift. Some mornings I’m tying my shoes and already thinking, “How am I going to stand there all day?” And then I start thinking about whether I remembered my name tag, which is ridiculous because it’s usually in the same place, but… yeah. I wouldn’t say it comes and goes in some big noticeable way. It’s just kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the past where you felt completely better for a while, without feeling worn out or tired, before starting to feel this way again?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can have a better day here and there, like if work is slower or I didn’t have to be up as early, but I wouldn’t say I’ve felt completely better for a while.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in—it’s been this worn-out feeling almost every day. Even after a full night, I get up for my cashier shift and my body and my head are still dragging. Like I’ll be brushing my teeth and already thinking about how long the day is going to feel… then I’ll get distracted noticing we’re almost out of toothpaste, which isn’t the point, but—yeah. I don’t remember a real stretch where it just went away and then came back.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period where you felt completely normal and not tired at all, and then later went back to feeling worn out again?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / cashier / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Fatigue\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I feel worn out almost every day, like even after a full night's sleep my body and head are still dragging when I get up for my cashier shift.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think I’ve had a period where I felt totally normal like that. I mean, maybe I’ll have a day that’s a little easier, like if the store isn’t as busy or I don’t have to rush in the morning, but I came in because it’s more like I’m worn out almost every day.

Even after I sleep through the night, I get up for my cashier shift and my body and my head are still kind of dragging. Like I’ll be getting ready and thinking about standing at the register all day, and then I’m checking if I have my keys, my name tag, all that stuff… but no, I wouldn’t say I had a real stretch where I felt completely normal and then it came back.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F33.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I came in because I’ve just been feeling really worn out almost every day. Like, even if I get what should be a full night’s sleep, I wake up and my body and my head still feel like they’re dragging, especially when I have to get up for my cashier shift. \n\nAnd it’s weird because I’ll be standing there scanning stuff, and people buy the strangest combinations of things sometimes—like batteries, soup, and a birthday card—and I’m trying to act normal, but I just feel so tired underneath it all.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s been, I guess, about a year and three months now. It kind of started after our older relative moved in with us. I remember because we had to rearrange the whole house, and there were all these boxes in the hallway for weeks, and I kept tripping over this one lamp cord—sorry, yeah, around then. About a year and three months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. I mean, I get kind of worn down, and sometimes I’m not in a great mood, but “sad most of the time” feels… I’m not sure. Like at work, I’ll be bagging groceries and someone’s card won’t swipe, and then the line backs up, and I’m mostly just tired and kind of tense, not necessarily sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I still do things, like I’ll put something on TV or mess around on my phone, and sometimes it’s fine. It’s just hard to tell if I’m enjoying it the same way as before, you know?\n\nLike, the other night I started watching this cooking show, and they were making these tiny desserts that looked way too complicated, and then I got distracted thinking about how people even learn to do that for a living. So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some big change, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to find pleasurable but no longer enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think there’s anything where I’m like, “Oh, I used to love that and now I hate it.” It’s more like I’ll start something and get sidetracked.\n\nLike, I used to watch those home renovation shows, and I still will, but then I start thinking about how expensive tile is now, or whether the cashier at the hardware store has to memorize all those little plumbing parts—kind of random. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a clear thing I don’t enjoy anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're not getting as much pleasure out of things as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, it’s hard for me to measure that. Like, I’ll still watch stuff or eat something I like and it’s not like I feel nothing. Sometimes I think maybe I’m just distracted.\n\nLike yesterday I had one of those donuts from the grocery store bakery, the ones with the little chocolate stripes, and I started thinking about how they get them all looking the same. So I don’t know if I’m getting less pleasure, exactly. I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things that used to give you pleasure just don't seem as enjoyable anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think so in a clear way. I still get some enjoyment out of things, I think. It’s just hard to tell because sometimes I’ll be doing something I used to like and then my mind goes off somewhere else.\n\nLike I’ll put on a movie and then I’m suddenly wondering where they filmed it, or how many people are standing around behind the camera, and then I realize I missed half the scene. So I wouldn’t say things don’t seem enjoyable anymore… I just haven’t really noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve lost interest, not like completely. I still do the same kinds of things, I guess. Sometimes I don’t stick with them as long, but that might just be me getting distracted.\n\nLike I’ll start watching a video or something and then start wondering how the recommendation thing even picked it for me, and then I’m down some whole other thought. So… I haven’t really noticed a clear loss of interest, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling worn out?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything major, no. I mean, my pants seem to fit about the same, but I don’t weigh myself much. The scale at the grocery store used to be by the pharmacy, and I always wondered if people actually used those or if it was just kind of there… but yeah, I don’t think there’s been some big weight change that I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on things for as long as you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Especially at work. I’ll be at the register, looking right at the screen, and somehow I’m not really taking it in because I’m thinking about stuff at home or bills or whatever.\n\nThen I’ll have to recount the drawer, or I’ll catch some little mistake and think, “How did I miss that?” It’s not all the time, but it does happen more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great. Most nights I’ll get into bed already tired, but then my brain kind of starts making lists without asking me.\n\nI’ll be thinking about bills, or what my schedule is supposed to be, or whether everything’s okay at home. And then even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really settle down, if that makes sense. Like I was technically asleep, but not really rested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about things or like you're worthless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I feel bad if I mess something up, like anybody would. I’ll replay it in my head sometimes, but “worthless” sounds kind of strong.\n\nLike, I remember once at work this lady got really upset because a coupon wouldn’t scan, and I kept thinking about it after, even though it probably wasn’t my fault. Coupons are always weird anyway—some of them say one thing in tiny print and then the register says another thing.\n\nBut no, I wouldn’t say I feel worthless exactly. Guilt… maybe here and there, but not like all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're running on empty or lacking energy to do the things you need to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty often, honestly. That’s kind of one of the reasons I came in. I feel worn out almost every day, like even if I technically get a full night’s sleep, I wake up and my body’s already dragging.\n\nAnd then I’ve got to go stand at the register for hours, and it’s not like cashiering is digging ditches or anything, but by the time I’m there I feel like my head is slow, like I’m moving through mud. It’s weird because sometimes I’ll tell myself, “You slept, so why are you tired?” but I still feel empty, like there’s not much in the tank.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel irritable?\n\nPatient: Irritable… I’m not sure. I mean, I get annoyed sometimes, like when people are standing in line and the card reader’s doing that thing where it freezes, and everyone looks at me like I built the machine or something.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s more than normal. My dad used to get mad at machines too—he had this old printer that he’d smack on the side, which never helped. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed myself snapping at people a lot, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in your life where you felt really down or depressed that lasted for a while and then got better, only to happen again?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I’ve had times where I felt kind of low, like everybody does, but I don’t know if I’d call it a whole period of being depressed.\n\nIt’s hard to think back and sort it out. Like, some weeks at work just feel heavier than others, especially around holidays when people are stressed and the store plays the same songs over and over—there was one year I swear I heard the same jingle so many times I started hearing it when I got home.\n\nBut, uh… as far as it coming and going in a pattern, like episodes? I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep problems are contributing to how tired you feel during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I’ll be tired when I get into bed, like my body wants to shut off, but then my brain just starts doing inventory or something—bills, what shift I have coming up, whether everyone at home is okay.\n\nAnd then hours kind of slip by. Sometimes I do fall asleep, but it doesn’t feel like I ever really settle. It’s like the TV is off but still buzzing in the background—my grandma had this old set that did that, actually, drove everyone nuts. But yeah… I wake up feeling like I didn’t really get rest, even if I was technically asleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tiredness would improve if you were sleeping better?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it probably would. I mean, that’s kind of why I came in—because I feel worn out almost every day. Even if I get what should be a full night, I wake up and my body and my head are still kind of dragging when I have to go in for my cashier shift.\n\nIt’s weird, because I’ll be standing there scanning stuff and I’m thinking about how heavy my arms feel, and then someone buys like thirty cans of cat food and I’m counting them wrong—well, not wrong, I catch it, but you know what I mean. So… yeah, better sleep would help, I’d think.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the past where you felt more energetic and less tired for a while, before feeling worn out again?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve had, like, a clear stretch where I suddenly had a bunch more energy. It’s more that lately I just feel worn out almost every day.\n\nEven if I sleep what should be enough, I get up and my body and my head are still dragging before my cashier shift. Some mornings I’m tying my shoes and already thinking, “How am I going to stand there all day?” And then I start thinking about whether I remembered my name tag, which is ridiculous because it’s usually in the same place, but… yeah. I wouldn’t say it comes and goes in some big noticeable way. It’s just kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the past where you felt completely better for a while, without feeling worn out or tired, before starting to feel this way again?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can have a better day here and there, like if work is slower or I didn’t have to be up as early, but I wouldn’t say I’ve felt completely better for a while.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in—it’s been this worn-out feeling almost every day. I mean, that’s kind of why I came in—because I feel worn out almost every day. Even if I get what should be a full night, I wake up and my body and my head are still kind of dragging when I have to go in for my cashier shift.\n\nIt’s weird, because I’ll be standing there scanning stuff and I’m thinking about how heavy my arms feel, and then someone buys like thirty cans of cat food and I’m counting them wrong—well, not wrong, I catch it, but you know what I mean. So… yeah, better sleep would help, I’d think.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the past where you felt more energetic and less tired for a while, before feeling worn out again?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve had, like, a clear stretch where I suddenly had a bunch more energy. It’s more that lately I just feel worn out almost every day.\n\nEven if I sleep what should be enough, I get up and my body and my head are still dragging before my cashier shift. Some mornings I’m tying my shoes and already thinking, “How am I going to stand there all day?” And then I start thinking about whether I remembered my name tag, which is ridiculous because it’s usually in the same place, but… yeah. I wouldn’t say it comes and goes in some big noticeable way. It’s just kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the past where you felt completely better for a while, without feeling worn out or tired, before starting to feel this way again?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can have a better day here and there, like if work is slower or I didn’t have to be up as early, but I wouldn’t say I’ve felt completely better for a while.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in—it’s been this worn-out feeling almost every day. Even after a full night, I get up for my cashier shift and my body and my head are still dragging. Like I’ll be brushing my teeth and already thinking about how long the day is going to feel… then I’ll get distracted noticing we’re almost out of toothpaste, which isn’t the point, but—yeah. I don’t remember a real stretch where it just went away and then came back.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt completely normal and not tired at all, and then later went back to feeling worn out again?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had a period where I felt totally normal like that. I mean, maybe I’ll have a day that’s a little easier, like if the store isn’t as busy or I don’t have to rush in the morning, but I came in because it’s more like I’m worn out almost every day.\n\nEven after I sleep through the night, I get up for my cashier shift and my body and my head are still kind of dragging. Like I’ll be getting ready and thinking about standing at the register all day, and then I’m checking if I have my keys, my name tag, all that stuff… but no, I wouldn’t say I had a real stretch where I felt completely normal and then it came back."
